I suppose it does build very quickly, within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to driving over bridges or do you experience similar feelings in other situations?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the bridges, yes—especially those long ones over the water. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years and people drive over them every day. Still, when I think about being out over all that open water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges triggered by the thought of the bridge itself or by the actual act of driving on it?\n\nPatient: It’s more the actual driving onto it, I think. I can think about the bridge and not feel wonderful, but what really sets it off is when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Then, almost right away, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—even though I know there’s nowhere to go. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than driving over bridges?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. It’s gotten uncomfortable enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s mostly when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost right away my chest tightens, and my hands grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling like I have to get off immediately… even though I know there isn’t really anywhere to go. I suppose it does build very quickly, within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to driving over bridges or do you experience similar feelings in other situations?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the bridges, yes—especially those long ones over the water. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years and people drive over them every day. Still, when I think about being out over all that open water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges triggered by the thought of the bridge itself or by the actual act of driving on it?\n\nPatient: It’s more the actual driving onto it, I think. I can think about the bridge and not feel wonderful, but what really sets it off is when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Then, almost right away, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—even though I know there’s nowhere to go. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than driving over bridges?\n\nPatient: No, not really… not in the same way. It’s mostly tied to those bridges, especially thinking about driving over all that open water. I know they’re safe, and I know people cross them every day, so I feel a little silly saying it, but the fear just feels much bigger than the actual danger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving over bridges because of the fear you experience?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do avoid them, more than I like to admit. I’ve turned down some gallery openings, birdwatching outings, even a few visits with my daughter if it meant I’d have to drive over those bridges myself. If someone else can drive, I’m much more willing to go.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been the one who planned things and managed things on my own. I don’t like feeling as though I’m making a fuss.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

No, I don’t think so… not like that. It’s really when my car gets to one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost immediately my chest tightens, and I grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—though of course there’s nowhere to get off.

Yes… I think it’s mostly the open water. I know, logically, those bridges have been there for years, and people drive over them all day without anything happening. I do understand that.

I think it’s more the water, really. The height may be part of it, but what gets to me is seeing all that open water underneath and around me.

I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without thinking twice. But when I’m the one driving over all that water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it may sound a little silly, but I’ve been having trouble when I’m driving over those long bridges toward Miami. The moment my car gets onto one, my chest tightens and my hands just clamp down on the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely sure I need to get off right away—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. It’s gotten uncomfortable enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s mostly when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost right away my chest tightens, and my hands grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling like I have to get off immediately… even though I know there isn’t really anywhere to go. I suppose it does build very quickly, within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to driving over bridges or do you experience similar feelings in other situations?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the bridges, yes—especially those long ones over the water. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years and people drive over them every day. Still, when I think about being out over all that open water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges triggered by the thought of the bridge itself or by the actual act of driving on it?\n\nPatient: It’s more the actual driving onto it, I think. I can think about the bridge and not feel wonderful, but what really sets it off is when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Then, almost right away, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—even though I know there’s nowhere to go. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than driving over bridges?\n\nPatient: No, not really… not in the same way. It’s mostly tied to those bridges, especially thinking about driving over all that open water. I know they’re safe, and I know people cross them every day, so I feel a little silly saying it, but the fear just feels much bigger than the actual danger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving over bridges because of the fear you experience?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do avoid them, more than I like to admit. I’ve turned down some gallery openings, birdwatching outings, even a few visits with my daughter if it meant I’d have to drive over those bridges myself. If someone else can drive, I’m much more willing to go.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been the one who planned things and managed things on my own. I don’t like feeling as though I’m making a fuss.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of driving over bridges affected your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I’ve missed some of the senior-center exhibition meetings because getting there would mean dealing with those bridges, and I’ve had to rely on other people for trips to Miami more than I’d like.\n\nIt makes me feel a bit less independent, I suppose, and less connected to the things—and the family responsibilities—that used to give my week some shape. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these intense fear episodes when you were not driving over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not like that. It’s really when my car gets to one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost immediately my chest tightens, and I grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—though of course there’s nowhere to get off.\n\nI know that sounds a bit unreasonable, but that’s the moment it happens. Not just out of the blue somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about the bridges themselves, such as their height or being over water, that you think contributes to your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s mostly the open water. I know, logically, those bridges have been there for years, and people drive over them all day without anything happening. I do understand that.\n\nBut when I’m the one driving, and I think about being over all that water with no easy way off, the fear feels much larger than the actual danger. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that properly, but that seems to be the part that gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about driving, aside from the bridges, that triggers this kind of fear for you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, ordinary driving doesn’t do that to me. What finally pushed me to come in was that very specific moment when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. My chest tightens, my hands just clamp onto the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I have to get off—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go.\n\nI hope that makes sense. It’s not driving in general so much as that bridge moment.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges more about the height of the bridges or the fact that they are over water?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the water, really. The height may be part of it, but what gets to me is seeing all that open water underneath and around me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without thinking twice. But when I’m the one driving over all that water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.231, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it may sound a little silly, but I’ve been having trouble when I’m driving over those long bridges toward Miami. The moment my car gets onto one, my chest tightens and my hands just clamp down on the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely sure I need to get off right away—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. It’s gotten uncomfortable enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s mostly when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost right away my chest tightens, and my hands grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling like I have to get off immediately… even though I know there isn’t really anywhere to go. I suppose it does build very quickly, within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to driving over bridges or do you experience similar feelings in other situations?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the bridges, yes—especially those long ones over the water. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years and people drive over them every day. Still, when I think about being out over all that open water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges triggered by the thought of the bridge itself or by the actual act of driving on it?\n\nPatient: It’s more the actual driving onto it, I think. I can think about the bridge and not feel wonderful, but what really sets it off is when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Then, almost right away, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—even though I know there’s nowhere to go. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than driving over bridges?\n\nPatient: No, not really… not in the same way. It’s mostly tied to those bridges, especially thinking about driving over all that open water. I know they’re safe, and I know people cross them every day, so I feel a little silly saying it, but the fear just feels much bigger than the actual danger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving over bridges because of the fear you experience?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do avoid them, more than I like to admit. I’ve turned down some gallery openings, birdwatching outings, even a few visits with my daughter if it meant I’d have to drive over those bridges myself. If someone else can drive, I’m much more willing to go.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been the one who planned things and managed things on my own. I don’t like feeling as though I’m making a fuss.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of driving over bridges affected your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I’ve missed some of the senior-center exhibition meetings because getting there would mean dealing with those bridges, and I’ve had to rely on other people for trips to Miami more than I’d like.\n\nIt makes me feel a bit less independent, I suppose, and less connected to the things—and the family responsibilities—that used to give my week some shape. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these intense fear episodes when you were not driving over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not like that. It’s really when my car gets to one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost immediately my chest tightens, and I grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—though of course there’s nowhere to get off.\n\nI know that sounds a bit unreasonable, but that’s the moment it happens. Not just out of the blue somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about the bridges themselves, such as their height or being over water, that you think contributes to your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s mostly the open water. I know, logically, those bridges have been there for years, and people drive over them all day without anything happening. I do understand that.\n\nBut when I’m the one driving, and I think about being over all that water with no easy way off, the fear feels much larger than the actual danger. What finally pushed me to come in was that very specific moment when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. But when I’m the one driving over all that water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that happens when you're on a bridge, aside from driving, that triggers this kind of fear for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. It’s gotten uncomfortable enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s mostly when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost right away my chest tightens, and my hands grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling like I have to get off immediately… even though I know there isn’t really anywhere to go. I suppose it does build very quickly, within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to driving over bridges or do you experience similar feelings in other situations?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the bridges, yes—especially those long ones over the water. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years and people drive over them every day. Still, when I think about being out over all that open water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges triggered by the thought of the bridge itself or by the actual act of driving on it?\n\nPatient: It’s more the actual driving onto it, I think. I can think about the bridge and not feel wonderful, but what really sets it off is when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Then, almost right away, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—even though I know there’s nowhere to go. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than driving over bridges?\n\nPatient: No, not really… not in the same way. It’s mostly tied to those bridges, especially thinking about driving over all that open water. I know they’re safe, and I know people cross them every day, so I feel a little silly saying it, but the fear just feels much bigger than the actual danger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving over bridges because of the fear you experience?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do avoid them, more than I like to admit. I’ve turned down some gallery openings, birdwatching outings, even a few visits with my daughter if it meant I’d have to drive over those bridges myself. If someone else can drive, I’m much more willing to go.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been the one who planned things and managed things on my own. I don’t like feeling as though I’m making a fuss.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of driving over bridges affected your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I’ve missed some of the senior-center exhibition meetings because getting there would mean dealing with those bridges, and I’ve had to rely on other people for trips to Miami more than I’d like.\n\nIt makes me feel a bit less independent, I suppose, and less connected to the things—and the family responsibilities—that used to give my week some shape. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these intense fear episodes when you were not driving over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not like that. It’s really when my car gets to one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost immediately my chest tightens, and I grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—though of course there’s nowhere to get off.\n\nI know that sounds a bit unreasonable, but that’s the moment it happens. Not just out of the blue somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about the bridges themselves, such as their height or being over water, that you think contributes to your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s mostly the open water. I know, logically, those bridges have been there for years, and people drive over them all day without anything happening. I do understand that.\n\nBut when I’m the one driving, and I think about being over all that water with no easy way off, the fear feels much larger than the actual danger. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that properly, but that seems to be the part that gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about driving, aside from the bridges, that triggers this kind of fear for you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, ordinary driving doesn’t do that to me. What finally pushed me to come in was that very specific moment when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. My chest tightens, my hands just clamp onto the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I have to get off—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go.\n\nI hope that makes sense. It’s not driving in general so much as that bridge moment.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges more about the height of the bridges or the fact that they are over water?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the water, really. The height may be part of it, but what gets to me is seeing all that open water underneath and around me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without thinking twice. But when I’m the one driving over all that water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that happens when you're on a bridge, aside from driving, that triggers this kind of fear for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in any other situation.\n\nIt’s mostly when I’m the one driving and I realize I’m already out there on the bridge. I suppose if I were just sitting there as a passenger I might not love it, but I can’t say it triggers the same kind of thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this fear when you were walking or biking over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t really say that I have. I don’t do much walking or biking over bridges anyway, but I haven’t noticed that same fear in that kind of situation.\n\nIt’s really been more when I’m driving—if that distinction is helpful.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF40.231, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it may sound a little silly, but I’ve been having trouble when I’m driving over those long bridges toward Miami. The moment my car gets onto one, my chest tightens and my hands just clamp down on the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely sure I need to get off right away—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. It’s gotten uncomfortable enough that I thought I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s mostly when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost right away my chest tightens, and my hands grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling like I have to get off immediately… even though I know there isn’t really anywhere to go. I suppose it does build very quickly, within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to driving over bridges or do you experience similar feelings in other situations?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the bridges, yes—especially those long ones over the water. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years and people drive over them every day. Still, when I think about being out over all that open water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges triggered by the thought of the bridge itself or by the actual act of driving on it?\n\nPatient: It’s more the actual driving onto it, I think. I can think about the bridge and not feel wonderful, but what really sets it off is when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Then, almost right away, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—even though I know there’s nowhere to go. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than driving over bridges?\n\nPatient: No, not really… not in the same way. It’s mostly tied to those bridges, especially thinking about driving over all that open water. I know they’re safe, and I know people cross them every day, so I feel a little silly saying it, but the fear just feels much bigger than the actual danger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving over bridges because of the fear you experience?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do avoid them, more than I like to admit. I’ve turned down some gallery openings, birdwatching outings, even a few visits with my daughter if it meant I’d have to drive over those bridges myself. If someone else can drive, I’m much more willing to go.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been the one who planned things and managed things on my own. I don’t like feeling as though I’m making a fuss.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of driving over bridges affected your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I’ve missed some of the senior-center exhibition meetings because getting there would mean dealing with those bridges, and I’ve had to rely on other people for trips to Miami more than I’d like.\n\nIt makes me feel a bit less independent, I suppose, and less connected to the things—and the family responsibilities—that used to give my week some shape. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these intense fear episodes when you were not driving over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not like that. It’s really when my car gets to one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost immediately my chest tightens, and I grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—though of course there’s nowhere to get off.\n\nI know that sounds a bit unreasonable, but that’s the moment it happens. Not just out of the blue somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about the bridges themselves, such as their height or being over water, that you think contributes to your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s mostly the open water. I know, logically, those bridges have been there for years, and people drive over them all day without anything happening. I do understand that.\n\nBut when I’m the one driving, and I think about being over all that water with no easy way off, the fear feels much larger than the actual danger. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that properly, but that seems to be the part that gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about driving, aside from the bridges, that triggers this kind of fear for you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, ordinary driving doesn’t do that to me. What finally pushed me to come in was that very specific moment when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. My chest tightens, my hands just clamp onto the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I have to get off—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go.\n\nI hope that makes sense. It’s not driving in general so much as that bridge moment.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges more about the height of the bridges or the fact that they are over water?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the water, really. The height may be part of it, but what gets to me is seeing all that open water underneath and around me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without thinking twice. But when I’m the one driving over all that water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that happens when you're on a bridge, aside from driving, that triggers this kind of fear for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in any other situation.\n\nIt’s mostly when I’m the one driving and I realize I’m already out there on the bridge. I suppose if I were just sitting there as a passenger I might not love it, but I can’t say it triggers the same kind of thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this fear when you were walking or biking over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t really say that I have. I don’t do much walking or biking over bridges anyway, but I haven’t noticed that same fear in that kind of situation.\n\nIt’s really been more when I’m driving—if that distinction is helpful.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were on a bridge but not driving, such as if you were a passenger in a car or on a bus?\n\nPatient: I think it wouldn’t be quite the same if I weren’t driving. I mean, I’d still be uneasy, especially on those long bridges toward Miami, but what really seems to set it off is when my own car reaches the bridge and I’m the one responsible.\n\nThat’s when my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp onto the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling like I have to get off the bridge right away… even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. I know that probably sounds a little dramatic, but that’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of being on a bridge be significantly reduced if you were not in control of the vehicle, such as being on a train or a ferry that crosses the water?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced if I weren’t in control. I don’t want to overstate it, but the worst of it seems to happen when my car actually reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami.\n\nThat’s when I sort of… tense up right away. My chest tightens, my hands clamp onto the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I need to get off the bridge, even though I know there’s nowhere to go. So if it were a train or a ferry, I imagine it might still make me uncomfortable, but probably not in that same immediate way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be triggered by being in any situation where you feel trapped or unable to easily escape, not just driving over bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I haven’t really thought of it that broadly before.\n\nI don’t think I usually react that way in every situation where I can’t get out easily, at least not that I’ve noticed. It feels more tied to being in my car on those bridges. But maybe I’m not recognizing something—does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t like feeling as though I’m making a fuss.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of driving over bridges affected your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these intense fear episodes when you were not driving over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not like that. It’s really when my car gets to one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost immediately my chest tightens, and I grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—though of course there’s nowhere to get off.\n\nI know that sounds a bit unreasonable, but that’s the moment it happens. Not just out of the blue somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about the bridges themselves, such as their height or being over water, that you think contributes to your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s mostly the open water. I know, logically, those bridges have been there for years, and people drive over them all day without anything happening. I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve mostly noticed it in my own car, when I get to one of those long bridges toward Miami.

It’s really when my car gets to one of those long bridges toward Miami. It’s not driving in general so much as that bridge moment.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges more about the height of the bridges or the fact that they are over water?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the water, really. The height may be part of it, but what gets to me is seeing all that open water underneath and around me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without thinking twice. But when I’m the one driving over all that water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that happens when you're on a bridge, aside from driving, that triggers this kind of fear for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in any other situation.\n\nIt’s mostly when I’m the one driving and I realize I’m already out there on the bridge. I suppose if I were just sitting there as a passenger I might not love it, but I can’t say it triggers the same kind of thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this fear when you were walking or biking over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t really say that I have. I don’t do much walking or biking over bridges anyway, but I haven’t noticed that same fear in that kind of situation.\n\nIt’s really been more when I’m driving—if that distinction is helpful.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were on a bridge but not driving, such as if you were a passenger in a car or on a bus?\n\nPatient: I think it wouldn’t be quite the same if I weren’t driving. I mean, I’d still be uneasy, especially on those long bridges toward Miami, but what really seems to set it off is when my own car reaches the bridge and I’m the one responsible.\n\nThat’s when my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp onto the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling like I have to get off the bridge right away… even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. I know that probably sounds a little dramatic, but that’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of being on a bridge be significantly reduced if you were not in control of the vehicle, such as being on a train or a ferry that crosses the water?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced if I weren’t in control. I don’t want to overstate it, but the worst of it seems to happen when my car actually reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami.\n\nThat’s when I sort of… tense up right away. My chest tightens, my hands clamp onto the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I need to get off the bridge, even though I know there’s nowhere to go. So if it were a train or a ferry, I imagine it might still make me uncomfortable, but probably not in that same immediate way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be triggered by being in any situation where you feel trapped or unable to easily escape, not just driving over bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I haven’t really thought of it that broadly before.\n\nI don’t think I usually react that way in every situation where I can’t get out easily, at least not that I’ve noticed. It feels more tied to being in my car on those bridges. It’s mostly when my car gets onto one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost right away my chest tightens, and my hands grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling like I have to get off immediately… even though I know there isn’t really anywhere to go. I suppose it does build very quickly, within minutes.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear limited to driving over bridges or do you experience similar feelings in other situations?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s the bridges, yes—especially those long ones over the water. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I know perfectly well they’ve been there for years and people drive over them every day. Still, when I think about being out over all that open water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges triggered by the thought of the bridge itself or by the actual act of driving on it?\n\nPatient: It’s more the actual driving onto it, I think. I can think about the bridge and not feel wonderful, but what really sets it off is when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. Then, almost right away, my chest tightens, my hands clamp down on the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—even though I know there’s nowhere to go. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have these intense fear episodes in situations other than driving over bridges?\n\nPatient: No, not really… not in the same way. It’s mostly tied to those bridges, especially thinking about driving over all that open water. I know they’re safe, and I know people cross them every day, so I feel a little silly saying it, but the fear just feels much bigger than the actual danger. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding driving over bridges because of the fear you experience?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do avoid them, more than I like to admit. I’ve turned down some gallery openings, birdwatching outings, even a few visits with my daughter if it meant I’d have to drive over those bridges myself. If someone else can drive, I’m much more willing to go.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because I’ve always been the one who planned things and managed things on my own. I don’t like feeling as though I’m making a fuss.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of driving over bridges affected your relationships or daily activities in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has, though I don’t like to make it sound too dramatic. I’ve missed some of the senior-center exhibition meetings because getting there would mean dealing with those bridges, and I’ve had to rely on other people for trips to Miami more than I’d like.\n\nIt makes me feel a bit less independent, I suppose, and less connected to the things—and the family responsibilities—that used to give my week some shape. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had one of these intense fear episodes when you were not driving over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so… not like that. It’s really when my car gets to one of those long bridges toward Miami. Almost immediately my chest tightens, and I grip the wheel so hard, and I get this sudden feeling that I have to get off—though of course there’s nowhere to get off.\n\nI know that sounds a bit unreasonable, but that’s the moment it happens. Not just out of the blue somewhere else.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything about the bridges themselves, such as their height or being over water, that you think contributes to your fear?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it’s mostly the open water. I know, logically, those bridges have been there for years, and people drive over them all day without anything happening. I do understand that.\n\nBut when I’m the one driving, and I think about being over all that water with no easy way off, the fear feels much larger than the actual danger. I’m not sure if I’m explaining that properly, but that seems to be the part that gets to me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about driving, aside from the bridges, that triggers this kind of fear for you?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, ordinary driving doesn’t do that to me. What finally pushed me to come in was that very specific moment when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami. My chest tightens, my hands just clamp onto the wheel, and I suddenly feel absolutely certain I have to get off—even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go.\n\nI hope that makes sense. It’s not driving in general so much as that bridge moment.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of bridges more about the height of the bridges or the fact that they are over water?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the water, really. The height may be part of it, but what gets to me is seeing all that open water underneath and around me.\n\nI know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without thinking twice. But when I’m the one driving over all that water, the fear feels much bigger than the actual danger… if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else that happens when you're on a bridge, aside from driving, that triggers this kind of fear for you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. At least, I haven’t really noticed it in any other situation.\n\nIt’s mostly when I’m the one driving and I realize I’m already out there on the bridge. I suppose if I were just sitting there as a passenger I might not love it, but I can’t say it triggers the same kind of thing. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this fear when you were walking or biking over a bridge?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t really say that I have. I don’t do much walking or biking over bridges anyway, but I haven’t noticed that same fear in that kind of situation.\n\nIt’s really been more when I’m driving—if that distinction is helpful.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were on a bridge but not driving, such as if you were a passenger in a car or on a bus?\n\nPatient: I think it wouldn’t be quite the same if I weren’t driving. I mean, I’d still be uneasy, especially on those long bridges toward Miami, but what really seems to set it off is when my own car reaches the bridge and I’m the one responsible.\n\nThat’s when my chest tightens, and my hands just clamp onto the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling like I have to get off the bridge right away… even though, of course, there’s nowhere to go. I know that probably sounds a little dramatic, but that’s what finally made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of being on a bridge be significantly reduced if you were not in control of the vehicle, such as being on a train or a ferry that crosses the water?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would be reduced if I weren’t in control. I don’t want to overstate it, but the worst of it seems to happen when my car actually reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami.\n\nThat’s when I sort of… tense up right away. My chest tightens, my hands clamp onto the wheel, and I get this sudden feeling that I need to get off the bridge, even though I know there’s nowhere to go. So if it were a train or a ferry, I imagine it might still make me uncomfortable, but probably not in that same immediate way. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear would be triggered by being in any situation where you feel trapped or unable to easily escape, not just driving over bridges?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure. I haven’t really thought of it that broadly before.\n\nI don’t think I usually react that way in every situation where I can’t get out easily, at least not that I’ve noticed. It feels more tied to being in my car on those bridges. But maybe I’m not recognizing something—does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel a similar level of fear if you were in a situation where you couldn't easily escape, but it wasn't related to driving or bridges?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least, I can’t think of an example like that. I don’t usually feel that same level of fear just because I can’t easily leave somewhere.\n\nIt really seems to be more connected to the bridge situation, especially being in the car. But I may not be answering it perfectly—if there’s a kind of situation you mean, I can try to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Would you feel the same level of fear if you were driving a different type of vehicle over a bridge, such as a truck or a motorcycle?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I’ve mostly noticed it in my own car, when I get to one of those long bridges toward Miami.\n\nI imagine the type of vehicle might change it a little, but the part that scares me is that moment when I’m on the bridge and I feel stuck there. My chest tightens, I grip the wheel, and I get that feeling like I have to get off right away, even though I can’t.\n\nSo… I think it would probably still happen if I were the one driving, but I don’t know if it would be exactly the same. Is that the kind of answer you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of being trapped or unable to escape is limited to driving over bridges or could it potentially be triggered by other situations?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly the bridges, at least that’s where I notice it. I know perfectly well those bridges have been there for years, and plenty of people cross them every day without any problem, so I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nBut when I think about driving over all that open water, the fear just feels much bigger than the actual danger. I suppose that “trapped” feeling is part of it, but I can’t say I’ve really felt it the same way in other situations. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of bridges be triggered if you were on a bridge in a different mode of transportation, such as a bus or a train?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really tested it on a bus or a train, so I don’t want to overstate it. What I do know is that it happens when my car reaches one of those long bridges toward Miami.\n\nIt’s almost immediate—my chest tightens, my hands clamp onto the wheel, and I suddenly feel like I have to get off the bridge right then, even though of course there’s nowhere to go. That’s really what finally made me come in. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
